# Freedom is Not Free: Platform Homing Policy and User Participation in Two-Sided Markets

Jizhou Lu,<sup>1</sup> Nina Yan,<sup>2</sup> Huigang Liang<sup>3</sup>

<sup>1</sup>Business School, Central University of Finance and Economics, China, jizhoulu@cufe.edu.cn <sup>2</sup>Business School, Central University of Finance and Economics, China, yannina@cufe.edu.cn <sup>3</sup>Department of Management Information Systems, University of Memphis, USA, huigang.liang@gmail.com

## Abstract

Pricing and non-pricing differentiation are often jointly used in the platform economy, yet prior research has largely focused on each strategy individually rather than jointly. Our study aims to bridge the gap in current research by examining the interplay between platform pricing and nonpricing differentiation strategies, considering the impact of platform homing policies and asymmetrical network effects. We developed a stylized game-theoretical model to study pricing decisions and user loyalty, which account for various non-pricing (i.e., service) settings and platform homing policies that impact user freedom. Our study generates two insights. First, freedom of platform participation comes at a cost. We demonstrate that platforms can apply two distinct strategies to maximize gains: pay-for-freedom and freedom-premium. Depending on the specific network effect, platforms may choose to charge users for their freedom to participate on multiple platforms concurrently, or they may opt to subsidize users when restricting their freedom. Second, service levels can significantly affect user participation incentives and loyalty, suggesting that platforms can better manage customer relationships by setting an appropriate service level. In particular, while an increase in the service level can increase the number of user participants, there are conflicting interside interplatform spillover effects and opposite interside intraplatform effects on user loyalty at different levels of user freedom.

Keywords: Two-Sided Market, Platform, Pricing, Service, Game Theory, Homing Policy

Zhijie Lin was the accepting senior editor. This research article was submitted on August 16, 2024, and underwent three revisions. Nina Yan is the corresponding author.

## 1 Introduction

Two-sided platforms enable two or more distinct user groups to interact with each other for value-added services (Leong et al., 2019). Examples include riders matching with drivers on Uber, buyers and sellers transacting on Alibaba, and guests connecting with hosts on Airbnb. Platform business models represent a fastgrowing portion of digital economy growth (Elliott et al., 2018), evidenced by the increasing prevalence and market capitalization of platform companies. For instance, platform companies among the top 10 companies globally rose from three in 2015 (total market cap: \$1.434 trillion) to seven in 2021 (total market cap: \$8.987 trillion) (PwC, 2015, 2021).

In a recent lawsuit filed by Uber Technologies against DoorDash (Haddon, 2025), Uber alleged that DoorDash employs anti-competitive tactics by pressuring restaurants into exclusive partnerships. Specifically, Uber claimed that DoorDash threatens restaurants with higher commission rates if they choose to also list their services on the competing Uber Eats platform. Uber argued that these alleged coercive practices harm the market by artificially inflating prices for consumers and lowering service quality. Platforms may leverage strategies such as exclusivity requirements to achieve greater control over their user base. In this case, these arrangements allow them to forge direct, exclusive relationships with restaurants, thereby indirectly influencing and controlling customer behavior. This choice relates to controlling “homing” behavior— whether users engage with a single platform (“singlehoming”) or multiple platforms (“multi-homing”). DoorDash’s alleged actions discourage multi-homing among restaurants. Furthermore, this case highlights that competition between these platforms extends beyond simple price wars. Non-price factors, such as the level of service offered and strategic decisions about exclusivity, are significant differentiators that influence restaurant loyalty, customer experience, and the overall competitive landscape.

A two-sided platform could enforce multi-homing or single-homing to regulate user participation. Multihoming happens when customers or service providers participate on multiple platforms at the same time (Zhu & Iansiti, 2019). For example, a restaurant may offer services on multiple food delivery platforms, and a consumer can use different hotel booking platforms to find the best offer. In contrast, single-homing forces users to transact with only one platform. Different homing policies reflect different levels of exclusivity from the perspective of platforms and correspondingly different levels of freedom from the user’s perspective. When implementing a multi-homing policy, it is challenging for platforms to attain dominant market shares and strong network effects (Cusumano et al., 2020). Thus, some scholars suggest that platforms should limit user freedom by forbidding multi-homing (Fijneman et al., 2018); possible ways of doing so include imposing contractual terms directly and utilizing price structures indirectly (OECD, 2018). For example, Amazon offers incentives such as free advertising and lower commissions to merchants who agree to stop selling on Temu (Stambor, 2025). Likewise, Alibaba Group implemented similar constraints several years ago (Yu, 2021). Moreover, exclusive partnerships serve as an effective mechanism to implement single-homing. For instance, 24S, launched in 2017, is the exclusive online partner of luxury brands such as Louis Vuitton, Dior, Celine, and Fendi.<sup>1</sup> Over 20 brands—including Decathlon, Timberland, TEENIE WEENIE, and those under the Inditex Group—have established exclusive collaborations with Tmall, making it their sole third-party e-commerce partner. 2 Consequently, consumers who wish to purchase these products online must do so through these specific platforms, thereby becoming single-homed. Furthermore, a platform could implement different homing policies on both sides and strike a balance between freedom levels for the two sides. Prior research has found that if platforms reduce users’ freedom by enforcing single-homing on one side, they may have to increase users’ freedom by multihoming on the opposite side (Zhu & Iansiti, 2019). However, it remains to be seen whether and to what extent platforms can reap financial benefits from the freedom they offer to users. This leads to our first research question: How can platforms charge users for freedom on either side of the platform to maximize benefits?

Furthermore, the lawsuit between Uber and DoorDash implies that platforms distinguish themselves not only by pricing but also in the services they provide. Service offerings represent a strategic choice of platforms engaged in competition, requiring substantial effort and investment that cannot easily be modified. One example of a successful differentiation strategy is JD.com’s logistics service, which necessitated significant investment and enabled the company to distinguish itself from Alibaba (McKevitt, 2018). Similarly, Airbnb has spent millions of dollars to differentiate itself by enhancing its safety-related services (Carville, 2021). Service differentiation can be achieved in two ways. First, two competing platforms of a two-sided market may provide different services to the same side of the market. For example, transaction platforms such as Walmart and Amazon deliver products to consumers through different logistics services (Garland, 2024). These two types of delivery may result in different levels of customer satisfaction. Second, a platform may provide different services to different sides of the platform. For example, Damai, the counterpart of Ticketmaster in China, delivers differentiated services to both sides of its platform. On the consumer side, it enhances user experience through personalized event recommendations, integrated shopping features, and virtual reality (VR) previews of sold-out events. On the supplier and agent side, Damai offers advanced tools such as real-time sales monitoring, customizable visual reports, and collaborative resource-sharing partnerships (e.g., joint promotions with Lakala) (Leong et al., 2019). However, it is unclear how service differentiations influence platforms’ operational decisions. This leads to our second research question: Given existing service level asymmetry, how can platforms optimize pricing to maximize benefits?

Not only is the service asymmetric, but the strength of network effects also varies across the two sides, which fuels the rapid growth of two-sided platforms (Kumar et al., 2018; Tan et al., 2020; Pu et al., 2022). Network effects (specifically, cross-side network effects) refer to the phenomenon in which the more buyers who join a platform, the more attractive it becomes for sellers to join, and vice versa (Hagiu & Wright, 2024). The strength of network effects can vary dramatically in shaping value creation and capture. In 2021, the food delivery platform Meituan reported 690 million transaction users, representing a 35.2% increase from the previous year. The number of active merchants on the platform reached 8.8 million, marking a 29% yearover-year growth rate.<sup>3</sup> Between 2021 and 2022, For ride sharing platform Didi, its total passenger base increased from 493 million to 587 million, a growth of approximately 19.07%, while the number of drivers on the platform rose from 15 million to 23 million, an increase of about 53.33%. <sup>4</sup> These discrepancies illustrate the asymmetric network effects on the two sides of a two-sided platform. The same phenomenon is also observed on other types of platforms, such as online dating platforms (Voigt & Hinz, 2015). These asymmetries critically influence pricing and platform strategies, yet their profit implications remain underexplored. Accordingly, our third research question is: How can platforms address the asymmetric strengths of the network effects of a platform’s two sides to maximize profits through optimal pricing?

To answer these questions, we developed a stylized game-theoretic model of a platform duopoly. We examined how homing policies, service differentiation, and asymmetric network effects jointly shape optimal pricing under symmetric (single- or multi-homing on both sides) and asymmetric (single-homing on one side, multi-homing on the other) regimes. Our analysis yielded actionable insights for platform managers seeking to balance user freedom, service investment, and network dynamics to drive profitability.

Our research generated several major findings. First, our study reveals that freedom is not free. Freedom emerges as an intangible asset within the platform economy.<sup>5</sup> Our study not only substantiates the theoretical value of freedom but also quantifies its economic worth. Providing more freedom to users for participation poses challenges for the platform in realizing benefits. Consequently, to strike a balance between gains and losses, the platform needs to use positive (or negative) financial incentives to compensate for reduced (or enhanced) freedom. Specifically, if a platform adopts a multi-homing policy for both sides, it should implement a pay-for-freedom strategy, refraining from offering subsidies to users on either side. Conversely, if the platform opts for a single-homing policy for one or both sides, it needs to purchase users’ freedom. A freedompremium strategy should be adopted to provide subsidies to all users, including those on the multihoming side. Our findings shed light on the strategic pricing choices available to platforms based on their homing policies.

Second, our study reveals intriguing spillover effects in the platform market based on homing policies, i.e., interside intraplatform and interside interplatform spillover effects. The former means that in a hybrid homing policy (i.e., the medium freedom scenario), increased service on one side of the focal platform consistently enhances user loyalty on the other side, showcasing a positive interplay between service quality and allegiance. However, under complete freedom (i.e., multi-multi homing policy), elevated service on one side may decrease loyalty on the other side. In contrast, interside interplatform spillover effects reveal an opposite trend, where user loyalty to the focal platform’s multi-homing side increases when the rival platform enhances service on the other side under a complete freedom environment, but decreases under a medium freedom environment. These insights offer valuable guidance for platform operations, emphasizing the complexity of user loyalty amid varying service levels and homing policies.

Third, our study uncovers an interesting relationship between the platform’s service level and user participation, revealing an augmentation effect. The platform’s service level consistently exerts a nonnegative influence on users’ participation levels. Whether the platform adopts a single- or multi-homing policy, an increase in the service level leads to a rise in the number of users participating on the platform when users engage in partial participation, while the number remains constant when users engage in full participation. This insight offers a deeper understanding of the intricate interactions between service levels, user participation, and platform profitability in the dynamic landscape of two-sided markets.

Our contributions are threefold. First, we extend prior research by comprehensively examining platform differentiation strategies encompassing both pricing and non-pricing aspects. This paper is among the first to holistically consider both dimensions, providing guidance for two-sided platforms in making informed differentiation decisions. Second, our research enriches the understanding of pricing strategies employed by two-sided platforms by factoring in the asymmetrical influences of network effects and varying service levels. We place particular emphasis on how these platforms charge users for their participation freedom and how they compensate for constraints on user freedom. Consequently, our study brings into sharp focus the underlying theoretical principles that illuminate when and why user freedom is not free within the context of the platform economy. Third, our study explores user participation freedom and loyalty

digital platforms. (https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/ social-sciences/platform-economy)

in the context of two-sided markets, accounting for differences in platform service offerings and network effect strengths. We discover that user loyalty is intricately linked to the levels of user freedom dictated by the platform’s homing policies.

This paper is organized as follows. Section 2 reviews the literature on platform exclusivity and platform differentiation. In Section 3, we describe the model setup. In Section 4, we analyze the research questions in a duopoly market. Then, we discuss incentives for user participation, platform pricing strategies, and social welfare in Section 5. Section 6 concludes the study and suggests directions for further research.

## 2 Literature Review

Our research builds primarily on the theoretical literature on two-sided markets, which mainly aims to facilitate transactions between different organizations, entities, and individuals (Bonina et al., 2021). In the last decade, many models have been developed to address diverse issues related to two-sided markets (Armstrong, 2006; Belleflamme & Peitz, 2019; Cosnita-Langlais et al., 2021; Hagiu, 2009; Hinz et al., 2020; Rietveld & Schilling, 2021; Taeuscher & Rothe, 2021). The two streams most relevant to our study are platform homing policy and platform differentiation.

## 2.1 Platform Homing Policy

Acquiring and retaining users is critical for platforms operating in a market with multiple players, and a common strategy is implementing a homing policy. In a seminal study, Armstrong (2006) systematically analyzed the issue of single-homing and multi-homing in two-sided markets. Based on the assumption that at least one side of the platform is single-home and the user population on this side is constant, he explored the determinants of equilibrium prices, including cross-side network effects, user payment, and user participation, and found that platforms have incentives to mitigate network effects. Following Armstrong (2006), more researchers have studied homing policies from different perspectives. For example, Hagiu (2009) provided a new modeling framework to explore competitive platforms’ multi-homing policies for both sides and examined the effects of scale economies in multihoming and market expansion on equilibrium. Belleflamme and Peitz (2019) compared a market with single-homing on one side and multi-homing on the other side to another market with single-homing on both sides and explored the allocative effects of a change from single-homing to multi-homing. They found that platforms can benefit from imposing a multi-homing policy and that single-homers may benefit from the exclusivity. Bakos and Halaburda (2020) analyzed platform competition when agents multi-home on both sides and compared models that assume single-homing or multi-homing on at least one side. They found that when both sides multi-home, the strategic interdependence between the two sides of the same platform will diminish or even disappear. In addition, some studies have explored the differences in platform strategies between single-homing and multi-homing policies. For example, in the sharing economy context, Bernstein et al. (2021) examined the equilibrium prices on a ride sharing platform under consideration of the customers’ and drivers’ sensitivity to payment and congestion. Through comparative analysis between providing single-homing and multi-homing strategies, they found that under the single-homing policy, the equilibrium price is lower, and both consumers and drivers are better off. Barua and Mukherjee (2021) explored consumers’ adoption level of competing products in two-sided markets and found that firms can obtain more profit when competing in a highly competitive market under a multi-homing policy than under a single-homing policy.

Extending this line of research, our study comprehensively examines the homing policies provided by a platform by considering three scenarios: single-homing policies on both sides, a single-homing policy on one side and a multi-homing policy on the other side, and multi-homing policies on both sides. These three scenarios represent users’ participation freedom in the market, i.e., from no freedom to absolute freedom. We comparatively analyzed these policies in terms of platform pricing decisions and user participation actions.

## 2.2 Platform Differentiation

Another crucial way for the two-sided platform to acquire users is through differentiation. In the competitive market, platforms aim to outperform rivals through the differentiation of pricing and non-pricing strategies (Dushnitsky et al., 2022).

Regarding pricing differentiation, Teh et al. (2023) analyzed how platforms compete on transaction fees charged differently on both sides. They found that the fee structure shifts in favor of buyers when they are single-homers but shifts in favor of sellers when buyers are multi-homers. Bakos and Halaburda (2020) formulated a platform competition model based on the Hotelling line in a differentiated setting with a relaxed assumption of costless platform adoption. They showed that differentiated subsidy strategies for different groups could maximize the platform’s profit. In addition, Tan and Zhou (2021) studied price competition by utilizing the simplicity of the equilibrium pricing formula. They found that the prices and platform profits can increase when platform competition intensifies because of crossside externalities. Yuan et al. (2022) studied how software platforms select between penetration and skimming pricing strategies under asymmetric crossside network effects. They showed that differences in network effects influence platforms’ pricing decisions and shape their competitive differentiation. Furthermore, Hu and Liu (2023) considered a two-sided market competition problem where two platforms competed on both the supply and demand sides and studied the impact of precommitments on the equilibrium outcomes. They showed that in commission and capacity precommitment, the platforms had different optimal strategies to gain the most profitable outcomes, which were deeply affected by the competition.

Regarding non-pricing strategies, although several studies have examined platform competition in twosided markets from different perspectives, such as platforms’ preannouncement strategies (Chellappa & Mukherjee, 2021; Hu & Liu, 2023), the substitutability of targeting capabilities (Zhang et al., 2022), IT capabilities (Tan et al., 2015), and advertising (Chatterjee & Zhou, 2021; Dukes et al., 2022), service differentiation is one of the most salient competitive elements (Bar-Gill, 2019; Jullien & Sand-Zantman, 2021). Boston Consulting Group’s extensive research on 40 digital platforms revealed that the orchestrator could increase revenues by commanding a premium price and generating additional income from integrated services (Lang et al., 2019). Bhargava and Choudhary (2004) developed an economic model of infomediary services in which the infomediary platform determined optimal quality levels and pricing strategies, and buyers and sellers made decisions to participate. Under the consideration of positive indirect network externalities, they found that intermediaries have strong incentives to provide services at different levels of quality. Lin et al. (2012) discussed vertical differentiation in both monopoly and duopoly settings through a gametheoretical model, in which online service providers offered an ad-free service, an ad-supported service, or a combination of both. They found that competition can drive down both firms’ prices for the ad-support service, and competing firms can avoid the price war through differentiation if the advertising revenue rates are low. Furthermore, Chen et al. (2016) focused on the revenue model of online trading platforms, which provided different services (i.e., advertising or brokerage). Further, Dushnitsky et al. (2022) empirically examined the association between strategic choices and the performance of crowdfunding platforms, in which nonpricing strategies involved accessibility, inclusivity, and bundling.

Despite the considerable research on platform pricing and non-pricing differentiation strategies, few studies have examined their combined impact on platform competition. Our study fills this gap by theoretically examining how a platform’s pricing decisions, along with exogenously determined but adjustable service levels, jointly shape the user participation mode and platform profit maximization.

## 3 Model Setup

We consider three groups of agents in our model. On the supply side, firms provide products or services. On the demand side, customers purchase products or services. Between these two sides, two competing platforms interact with both sides. In our model, we generalize the supply and demand sides as user groups with structurally similar utility functions. Although the utility functions are symmetric in form, the actual service levels and prices offered to the two user groups may differ, resulting in asymmetric outcomes. (See Equation 1 for details.) We describe the users and platforms as follows and summarize the notations in Table 1.

Following the widely-used Hotelling model (e.g., Amaldoss et al., 2024; Bakos & Halaburda, 2020; Barua & Mukherjee, 2021; Bernstein et al., 2021; Cai & Jiang, 2020; Foros et al., 2024; Li & Zhu, 2021; Park et al., 2021), the two platforms $( j \in \{ A , B \} )$ are exogenously located at either end of the line [0,1] and simultaneously choose the optimal prices $( \mathrm { i . e . , } p _ { i , j } ^ { k * } )$ for each user under the homing policy ?? $( k \in \{ S S , S M , M M \} )$ . The platform charges a service fee to the users if $p _ { i , j } ^ { k } > 0$ , and offers free service if $p _ { i , j } ^ { k } \leq 0$ According to the standard assumptions in the related literature (e.g., Amaldoss et al., 2024; Foros et al., 2024; Luo et al., 2022), users are uniformly distributed along the Hotelling line (see Figures 1 and 2), and each user is willing to purchase, at most, one product in the market, with a unit misfit cost (denoted as ??). Consequently, a user located at ?? incurs a misfit cost of ???? when joining platform ??, and of $t ( 1 - x )$ when joining platform ??.

Facing the platforms’ various homing policies, users have different participation modes: using only one platform or using two platforms simultaneously. Accordingly, the users can be divided into two categories: single-homers and multi-homers. Singlehomers can exist when the platform implements ????, ????, and ???? because there are a number of users $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , n _ { i , j } ^ { k } )$ only participating on platform ?? even if they are allowed to multi-home. On the other hand, multi-homers can exist when the platform implements ???? and ???? . We describe the utility functions of single-homers and multi-homers in Equation (1) and Equation (2), respectively. In particular, the utility function of the single-homer on side ?? joining platform ?? under the homing policy $k \left( k \in \left\{ S S , S M , M M \right\} \right)$ is formulated as Equation (1).

$$
U _ {i, j} ^ {k} = V _ {i} + \alpha_ {i} N _ {- i, j} ^ {k} + \theta_ {i} s _ {i, j} ^ {k} - p _ {i, j} ^ {k} - t x _ {j},\tag{1}

where $i , - i \in \{ X , Y \} ( i \neq - i ) , j \in \{ A , B \} , x _ { A } = x$ and $x _ { B } = 1 - x . \alpha _ { i } \left( \alpha _ { i } > 0 \right)$ denotes the positive cross-side network effect of side ??, which measures the extra utility a user of side ?? gains from each additional user added to side −?? . $V _ { i }$ denotes a user’s base utility on side ?? . In this study, service levels are assumed to be exogenous to focus on how platforms adjust pricing in response to given service asymmetries. In addition, we use $\theta _ { i }$ to characterize the user’s sensitivity to the perceived service level provided by the platform, namely the preference coefficient. Equation (1) suggests that the user’s net utility increases with the network effect and the service level but decreases with the service fee and the distance to the platform. Single-homers will join the platform if its utility is positive, i.e., $U _ { i , j } ^ { k } > 0$

Users on each side choose to participate on either platform ?? or ?? according to their utilities, influenced by the level of service provided by the platform and the price charged for the service. They choose platform ?? if their utility is higher than joining platform $- j$ Specifically, we use pairs of ?? After each user decides on its participation action by checking its utility, the degree of user participation on both sides of the market will result in four scenarios (i.e., ????, ??ℱ, ℱ??, ?????? ℱℱ ). For either platform, ???? denotes users on both sides ?? and −?? partially participate, i.e., $N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } \in [ 0 , 1 )$ and $N _ { - i , j } ^ { k * } \in [ 0 , 1 ) ; \mathcal { P F }$ implies users on side ?? partially participate while users on side −?? fully participate, i.e., $N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } \in [ 0 , 1 )$ and $N _ { - i , j } ^ { k * } = 1 ; \mathcal { F P }$ refers to users on side ?? fully participating on side ?? and users on side −?? partially participating, i.e., $N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } = 1$ and $N _ { - i , j } ^ { k * } \in [ 0 , 1 )$ ; and ℱℱ refers to users on both sides fully participating, i.e., $N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } = N _ { - i , j } ^ { k * } = 1$

## 3.2 Platforms

Prior research, such as Bedre-Defolie and Nitsche (2020) and Xie et al. (2021), provides examples of platforms that implement various homing policies. To further enhance our understanding, we supplement these with additional practical cases. The scenario where the platform adopts multi-homing policies on both sides is relatively common. However, when a platform restricts user freedom—either directly through contractual terms or indirectly via pricing or technological constraints— one side may be subject to single-homing while the other remains multi-homing. Examples of such arrangements include Uber and DoorDash, and Amazon and Alibaba, as discussed in Section 1. In contrast, platforms enforcing single-homing on both sides are less common but can often be found on platforms with exclusive resources. In these contexts, customers seeking access to exclusive products or services are effectively locked into a single platform, thereby engaging in single-homing behavior.

As aforementioned, we use the superscript ?? to represent all possible homing policies of the platform, i.e., ?? ∈ {????, ????, ????} . Specifically, ???? (single-single) denotes single-homing policies for both sides, ???? (single-multiple) is a hybrid homing policy that characterizes single-homing for one side and multihoming for the other side, and ???? (multiple-multiple) describes multi-homing policies for both sides. Accordingly, ????, ????, and ???? indicate that the users in the market have no freedom, partial freedom, and complete freedom, respectively. In particular, suppose that one platform implements a single-homing policy and the other implements a multi-homing policy on the same side. The users on this side must choose only one platform to participate on due to the single-homing restriction. Thus, we follow common practices to specify that the two platforms adopt the same homing policy on the same side. Figure 3 illustrates the three scenarios.

$N _ { - i , A B } ^ { k } = \left( n _ { - i , A } ^ { k } + N _ { - i , A B } ^ { k } \right) + \left( n _ { - i , B } ^ { k } + N _ { - i , A B } ^ { k } \right) - N _ { - i , A B } ^ { k } =$ $n _ { - i , A } ^ { k } + n _ { - i , B } ^ { k } + N _ { - i , A B } ^ { k }$ which is the total number of users in the market and is normalized to 1.

The profit of platform $j \left( j \in \left\{ A , B \right\} \right)$ is calculated as the revenue generated from the service provided to all users minus the service cost. It seeks to maximize its profit by optimizing the service fee charged for each side. Thus, the decision objective of platform ?? with homing policy ?? is formulated in Equation (3).

$$
\max _ {p _ {i, j} ^ {k}, p _ {- i, j} ^ {k}} \pi_ {j} ^ {k} \big (p _ {i, j} ^ {k}, p _ {- i, j} ^ {k} \big) = N _ {i, j} ^ {k} p _ {i, j} ^ {k} + N _ {- i, j} ^ {k} p _ {- i, j} ^ {k} - C \big (s _ {i, j} ^ {k}, s _ {- i, j} ^ {k} \big),\tag{3}
$$

$$
s. t. \left\{ \begin{array}{l} N _ {i, j} ^ {k} \in [ 0, 1 ] \\ N _ {- i, j} ^ {k} \in [ 0, 1 ] \end{array} \right.,
$$

where $i , - i \in \{ X , Y \} ~ . ~ C \big ( s _ { i , j } ^ { k } , s _ { - i , j } ^ { k } \big )$ denotes the total service cost which follows a commonly used quadratic form (e.g., Ferguson et al., 2006; Mukhopadhyay et al., 2009; Niu et al., 2016; Tang et al., 2018), i.e., $C \big ( s _ { i , j } ^ { k } , s _ { - i , j } ^ { k } \big ) = h _ { i } \big ( s _ { i , j } ^ { k } \big ) ^ { 2 } + h _ { - i } \big ( s _ { - i , j } ^ { k } \big ) ^ { 2 }$ , and cost factors $h _ { i }$ and $h _ { - i }$ capture the platform’s operational efficiency on side ?? and −??.

## 4.3 Hybrid Homing Policy (????)

Under the hybrid homing policy, i.e., single-homing on one side and multi-homing on the other side, user participation will be more complicated because the competition between platforms is reflected not only in price and service differentiations but also in homing policies on different sides (see Figure 3b). For users on a given side, even if one platform gives them the freedom to be multi-homers, as long as the competing platform restricts them to be single-homers, the users will have to pick one of the two platforms. Therefore, under ???? policy, both platforms provide a symmetric homing policy for users on the same side. Without loss of generality, we assume that side ?? is single-homing and that the other side −?? is multi-homing in this case.

(c) The optimal profit of platform ?? is $\pi _ { j } ^ { S M * } =$

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\frac {- \left[ - 4 \theta_ {- i} ^ {2} s _ {- i , j} ^ {S M 2} C ^ {2} - (8 t ^ {2} - \alpha_ {i} ^ {2} - 6 \alpha_ {i} \alpha_ {- i} - \alpha_ {- i} ^ {2}) (C + H) ^ {2} \right]}{1 6 t C ^ {2}} - h _ {i} (s _ {i, j} ^ {S M}) ^ {2} -} \\ & {h _ {- i} (s _ {- i, j} ^ {S M}) ^ {2}, \mathrm{where} C = 6 t ^ {2} - \alpha_ {i} ^ {2} - 4 \alpha_ {i} \alpha_ {- i} - \alpha_ {- i} ^ {2}} \\ & {\mathrm{and} H = 2 t \theta_ {i} \Delta s _ {i, j} ^ {S M} + \theta_ {- i} \Delta s _ {- i, j} ^ {S M} (\alpha_ {i} + \alpha_ {- i}).} \end{array}

Based on Lemmas 1-3, the users on sides ?? and −?? of the market will eventually give rise to four participation modes, i.e., ????, ??ℱ, ℱ??, and ℱℱ , according to their utilities under different homing policies. More descriptions and discussions about these lemmas and corresponding results are presented in Appendix A.

## 5 Analysis

Based on the above equilibrium analysis, we conducted a comprehensive comparative analysis among different homing policies that bestow different levels of user freedom. Our analysis is focused on four essential business issues: platform pricing strategy and corresponding profits, user participation incentives, user loyalty, and social welfare.

## 5.1 Platform Pricing Strategy and Profits

## 5.1.1 Platform Pricing Strategy

A platform could implement one of two pricing strategies: pay-for-freedom or freedom-premium.

## Proposition 1: Platform pricing strategy

(a) Pay-for-freedom strategy: If platform ?? implements the MM policy (i.e., the users have complete freedom to choose a platform), the service provided by platform j cannot be free (i.e., the platform will not subsidize users on either side). Namely, $p _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } > 0$ always holds.

(b) Freedom-premium strategy: If platform ?? implements the SS policy or hybrid homing policy (i.e., at least users on one side have no freedom), then it may offer free service to users on either side, i.e., $p _ { i , j } ^ { S S * } < 0$ within SS and $p _ { i , j } ^ { S M * } < 0$ within SM, conditioned on specific network effects. Otherwise, no subsidy will be offered.

When platform ?? implements different homing policies, the users have different levels of freedom to participate, i.e., they have the highest level of freedom under the ???? homing policy, a medium level of freedom under the hybrid homing policy, and the lowest level of freedom under the ???? homing policy. Proposition 1 shows that freedom is a valuable asset for both platforms and users, and those who want it must pay for it. In other words, freedom is not free: Users need to pay for their freedom to join platforms without restriction, and platforms must pay for their restrictions on users’ freedom.

Proposition 1 illuminates the inherent trade-off between user freedom and platform exclusivity. When a platform opts for the ???? policy, which allows users to join the rival platform, it must charge a fee for that freedom, i.e., the pay-for-freedom strategy. This phenomenon is observable in the ride sharing market, where drivers and passengers can utilize multiple platforms, a dynamic that reflects the implementation of ???? policy across the sector. For example, in the US, the two primary ride sharing platforms, Uber and Lyft, impose fees on both parties. Uber reportedly takes a 25% commission from drivers, while Lyft’s commission, though not publicly disclosed, has been anecdotally reported by drivers to be between 20% and 25%. <sup>7</sup> In addition to these commissions, both platforms charge various fees to riders. For instance, Uber collects booking fees and surcharges, <sup>8</sup> whereas Lyft levies a service $\mathrm { f e e . } ^ { 9 }$ The rationale behind this strategy may be that when users have the freedom to choose among multiple platforms, offering free access may encourage opportunistic behaviors, e.g., free-riding and diluting platform value. By setting a positive price, platforms can screen out lowvalue or non-committed users, ensuring that the participants demonstrate both a willingness to engage and an ability to pay. In this sense, the imposed fees serve as a signaling and screening mechanism to attract and maintain a committed and valuable user base.

Conversely, the platform could restrict user freedom in two ways: complete restriction by the ???? policy and partial restriction by the hybrid homing policy (????). In both cases, the freedom-premium strategy can be employed. Because freedom is valuable to users, the platform needs to subsidize users to buy out their freedom to join rival platforms. Surprisingly, in the case of a hybrid homing policy, we find that users on either side may get subsidies. While it is reasonable for the user under the single-homing side to be subsidized, it is counterintuitive that the users under the multihoming side would also obtain subsidies. In this case, the users on the multi-homing side get both freedom and financial rewards; how does the platform benefit from this arrangement?

When a two-sided platform aggregates numerous suppliers of standardized or comparable products or services (e.g., hotels, ride sharing, or online retail), consumers may be inclined to utilize the focal platform exclusively. In such instances, consumers generally prefer as many suppliers as possible on the platform, as a richer set of options directly enhances their utility. Consequently, from the platform’s perspective, a need arises to implement a multi-homing policy on the supplier side to entice more suppliers to join. The multihoming policy entails affording suppliers complete freedom to choose which platform to join. In this scenario, the focal platform must provide subsidies to entice an increased number of suppliers to participate. As more suppliers join, the utility for consumers is significantly boosted due to network effects. Subsequently, the focal platform can leverage this augmented utility to charge consumers higher prices, thereby generating profits.

In short, Proposition 1 illuminates the complexity of pricing strategies in two-sided markets, unveiling the intriguing interplay between user freedom, platform exclusivity, and platform pricing strategies. Note that all conditions in Proposition 1 can be found in Appendix B.4.

## 5.1.2 Platform Profits

The users in the two-sided markets will eventually give rise to four participation modes, i.e., ????, ??ℱ, ℱ??, and ℱℱ under different homing policies. The following proposition shows the impacts of freedom and network effect on the platform’s profit with different user participation modes.

Proposition 2: The impact of freedom and network effect on the platform’s profit is:

(a) In the participation mode $\mathcal { F F } , \pi _ { j } ^ { s s * } > \pi _ { j } ^ { M M * }$

(b) In the participation mode $\mathcal { F P }$ and $\mathcal { P F }$ ,

$$
\partial \big (\pi_ {j} ^ {M M *} - \pi_ {j} ^ {S S *} \big) / \partial \alpha_ {- i} \geq 0 \mathrm{and} \partial \big (\pi_ {j} ^ {M M *} - \pi_ {j} ^ {S M *} \big) / \partial \alpha_ {- i} \geq 0.
$$

Proposition 2a posits that in the scenario where users on both sides fully participate on platform $j \left( \mathcal { F } \mathcal { F } \right)$ , adopting the ???? policy leads to higher profits for platform ?? compared to the scenario where users have complete freedom (???? policy). When all users on both ends of a two-sided market have already engaged in the market, it implies that there will be no influx of new users entering the market. That is, the two platforms in the market must compete for users within a fixed user pool. If both platforms adopt the ???? policy, each platform can only attract a portion of users (since users are only allowed to join one platform). However, if both platforms adopt the ???? policy, each platform has the potential to acquire all users (since users are permitted to join multiple platforms). Intuitively, gaining more users should be advantageous for the platform, as an increased user base helps increase the user utility, competitiveness, and profitability of the platform due to the network effect. Furthermore, Proposition 1 shows that under the ???? policy, the prices set by the platform for either side consistently remain positive, while under the ???? policy, the platform may provide subsidies to either side. This results indicate that, under the ???? policy, the platform achieves higher profits.

However, Proposition 2a suggests a counterintuitive relationship between the provision of freedom and the acquisition of profit, highlighting that restricting user freedom under certain conditions can be more financially advantageous for the platform. Table B1 demonstrates that $p _ { i , j } ^ { S S ^ { * } } < 0$ holds if and only if $\alpha _ { - i } > t .$ . It also means $p _ { - i , j } ^ { S S * } > 0$ if and only if $\alpha _ { i } < t .$ . This signifies that when side −?? derives greater benefit from the participation of side ?? than vice versa, the platform opts to decrease prices on side ??. Simultaneously, the platform will raise prices on side −?? . Consequently, a platform might opt to subsidize one side of the market, even at the cost of incurring losses on that side, to enhance overall profits. This proposition is supported by practical evidence. For example, in 2024, the App Store generated \$103.4 billion in revenue, compared to \$46.7 billion for Google Play.<sup>10</sup> Although many apps (e.g., Instagram, Spotify, Telegram) are available on both Apple’s App Store and Google Play, their distribution structures differ fundamentally. Apple follows a “closed garden” strategy that restricts app distribution to its proprietary App Store and prohibits side-loading—that is, installing apps outside the official store—thereby forming a structurally ???? environment (Abbott, 2024; NTIA, 2023; European Commission, 2022). Apps developed for iOS must rely on Apple’s proprietary development tools and frameworks (e.g., Swift, Metal, ARKit), which are not compatible with non-Apple ecosystems without significant redevelopment. In contrast, the Android ecosystem follows an open model that supports multiple distribution channels—such as Google Play, Samsung Galaxy Store, and Amazon Appstore—and also allows side-loading. Although developers may need to adjust store-specific SDKs (e.g., for payment or authentication), the core codebase and architecture typically remain portable, reflecting an ????- type configuration that facilitates multi-homing by both users and developers.

Proposition 2b describes the impact of network effects on platform ??’s profits when users on one side fully participate, whereas users on the other side partially participate on the platform (i.e., $\mathcal { F P }$ or ??ℱ). In this scenario, the difference in profits between the implementation of the ???? policy and the ???? policy on the platform amplifies with the increase of network effects. Furthermore, the distinction in profits between the adoption of the ???? policy and the ???? policy on the platform also magnifies with the increase of network effects.

We use a region plot (Figure 4) to visually illustrate Proposition 2, providing a comparative analysis of platform profits under different homing policies and user participation modes in light of various network effects. Under different combinations of $\alpha _ { X }$ and $\alpha _ { Y }$ we compare the platform profits under three different policies—???? , ????, and ???? —to investigate which of these three policies would emerge as the dominant equilibrium. Each region in the region plot is labelled with a specific policy, meaning that in this region, the platform profit under this policy is higher than that of the other two policies. Specifically, Figure 4a shows that if users on both sides fully join the platform $( \mathrm { i . e . } ,$ ${ \mathcal { F F } } $ ), platform ?? can obtain the highest profit by implementing the ???? policy only when the network effects on both sides are exceptionally high; otherwise, the optimal homing policy for platform ?? is ???? . This suggests that as the network effect increases, the platform needs to consider returning some freedom to users on one side to boost revenue. Furthermore, Figure 4a implies that ???? policy is never the best choice for the platform regardless of the network effect. In Appendix Tables A1 and A3, we show that the service price charged by the platform under the ???? policy (i.e., $p _ { - i , j } ^ { S S * } \ )$ is independent of $\alpha _ { - i }$ (i.e., $\partial p _ { - i , j } ^ { S S * } / \partial \alpha _ { - i } = 0 \ \bar { ) }$ , whereas it increases with $\alpha _ { - i }$ under the SM policy $( \mathrm { i . e . , } \partial p _ { - i , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial \alpha _ { - i } > 0 )$ . That is, as network effects increase, the fees charged by the platform to the multi-homing side increase under the hybrid homing policy, resulting in more profits. Thus, a hybrid homing policy is more profitable than a single-homing policy for both sides when users fully participate with a higher network effect.

Moreover, Figures 4b and 4c show the platform’s profits under different homing policies when users’ participation mode is ℱ?? or ??ℱ . When users’ participation mode is ℱ?? , with the strengthening of network effects, the platform’s optimal strategies are in the sequence of ????, ????, ????, ???? . On the other hand, when users’ participation mode is ??ℱ , with the increasing network effects, the optimal platform strategies follow the sequence of ????, ????, ????, ????. This reveals two insights. First, the platform’s optimal homing policy depends on whether the single-homers or multi-homers fully or partially participate on the platform. Second, the ???? policy, which is never the best strategy if users adopt ℱℱ, can become optimal only if the network effects on both sides are extremely strong. High network effects imply that an increase in the number of users on one side of the platform will bring substantial utility to users on the other side. This characteristic demands particular attention under the ???? policy. In the ???? policy, users can freely choose the platform without any constraints. Consequently, users are naturally drawn to platforms with higher network effects, and more users on the platform create opportunities for higher profits.

In summary, Proposition 2 unveils the intricate interactions among user participation modes, network effects, and platform profits. It offers managerial insights regarding how strategic homing policies can enhance profitability in diverse user participation scenarios.

## 5.2 User Participation Incentives

Proposition 3: Regardless of the platform homing policy (i.e., ?? ∈ {????, ????, ????} ), the service level of the platform will increase the user participation level, i.e., $\partial N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } / \partial s _ { i , j } ^ { k } > 0$ always holds.

Providing services entails costs for firms, with higherquality services typically incurring greater expenses. In practice, some high-level services may involve higher costs that are passed on to users. However, in our model, the service level is exogenous and its cost is reflected indirectly through the price in users’ utility. While superior services can boost user satisfaction, the increased costs might exert a financial burden on users. This leads to an important question: Does a higher level of service lead to increased customer engagement? Proposition 3 unveils a common theme across homing policies: Improving the service level of the platform improves user participation, which we call the augmentation effect. Regardless of the platform’s homing policy, an upward shift in the service level consistently contributes to an increase in user participation. This trend suggests that users highly value service level, often prioritizing it over other considerations such as freedom constraints and costs. This proposition is corroborated by a survey showing that consumers are willing to pay more for better service (Hyken, 2021). Therefore, Proposition 3 suggests that platforms can strategically invest in improving service levels without fearing a decline in user participation.

Figure 5 illustrates the impact of the platform’s service levels on users’ participating modes under different homing policies.<sup>11</sup> The regions labeled ℱℱ, ????, ??ℱ, and ℱ?? (the first letter is user participation on side X, and the second letter is user participation on side Y) represent user participation modes with different combinations of $s _ { X , j } ^ { k }$ and $s _ { Y , j } ^ { k }$ , and NA is the corresponding region in which platform ?? has a nonpositive profit.

In Figure 5, we observe a shrinkage effect. From Figure 5a to Figure 5b, all four regions (ℱℱ, ????, ??ℱ, and ℱ??) become smaller. With the ???? homing policy (Figure 5a), the feasible region within which the platform can make non-negative profits covers most of the range of service levels on each side. In contrast, with the ???? homing policy (Figure 5b), the platform can only make profits when offering medium-level services on each side. For both ???? and ???? , if a platform only provides lower service levels, it will lose both users and revenues. However, at high service levels, the platform will still be profitable under the ???? homing policy, while it will be unable to make a profit under the ???? homing policy. There are two possible reasons for this. One is that the high cost associated with high service levels will outweigh the revenues from the number of users attracted by such services, leading to non-positive profits. The other is that, under the ???? policy, users enjoy unrestricted freedom to participate on multiple platforms. However, it is crucial to recognize that this freedom is not free. The platform will impose higher prices on users who have complete freedom. This pricing strategy, while reflecting the value placed on user freedom, introduces a potential drawback. The elevated prices associated with the ???? policy may discourage some users from participating, leading to a reduction in profits. Regarding the hybrid homing policy, Figure 5c illustrates the degree of user participation, assuming side X is single-homing and side Y is multi-homing. From Figure 5a to Figure 5c, with the increasing service level on side Y, the region of ???? becomes smaller, but the region of $\mathcal { P F }$ becomes larger. This is because the users on side Y are multihoming and have more freedom to participate on different platforms simultaneously. Thus, more users on side Y will be attracted to platform ?? if they enjoy a higher level of service. On the other hand, the $\mathcal { P F }$ region increases when the service level on side X is improved. This is because the number of users participating on side X increases with the service level provided by platform ?? on this side, and this increase will boost the number of user participants on platform $j ^ { \circ } \mathbf { s }$ side Y through the network effect. However, this increase and the network effect will work if and only if the service level of side Y is at least medium. Furthermore, as the regions of ??ℱ and ???? change, the regions of $\mathcal { F P }$ and ℱℱ become very small from Figure 5a to Figure 5c, and their locations in Figure 5c imply that the corresponding $s _ { X , j } ^ { S M }$ is relatively high. This means that whether the users on side Y engage in partial or full participation, the users on side X rarely participate fully. This is because the users on side X are still single-homing and willing to fully participate on the platform if and only if they can enjoy an exceptionally high service level.

## 5.3 User Loyalty

A survey of 5,088 small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in China found that among firms with online sales channels, 27.8% concentrate their online sales on a single platform, while 72.1% distribute their sales across multiple platforms (Tencent Research Institute, 2025). This observation highlights the heterogeneity in user homing behavior under hybrid or ???? policies. Although such policies grant users on the multi-homing side greater freedom to access multiple platforms, not all users opt to exercise this option. We define multi-homer loyalty to platform ?? as $\bar { L } _ { i , j } ^ { k * } = n _ { i , j } ^ { k * } / N _ { i , j } ^ { k * }$ , the proportion of the number of users on side ?? who only participate on platform ?? to the total number of users on side ?? of platform $j . \ L ^ { 1 2 }$ Due to $n _ { i , j } ^ { k * } \leq N _ { i , j } ^ { k * } , L _ { i , j } ^ { k * } = 1$ indicates complete loyalty because all users on side ?? only choose platform ??. According to the total user amount in Lemmas 2-3, the expressions of user loyalty in the scenarios of the multi-multi-homing $( \mathrm { i . e . , } k = M M )$ policy and the hybrid homing $( \mathrm { i . e . , } k = S M )$ policy are as follows.

$$
L _ {i, j} ^ {k *} = \left\{ \begin{array}{c c} 1 - \frac {- t ^ {2} \alpha_ {i} [ \alpha_ {- i} + \theta_ {- i} (s _ {- i , j} ^ {M M} + s _ {- i , j} ^ {M M}) ] + (2 t ^ {2} - \alpha_ {i} \alpha_ {- i}) \{t [ 2 \alpha_ {i} + \theta_ {i} (s _ {i , j} ^ {M M} + s _ {i , j} ^ {M M}) - 2 t ] + \alpha_ {i} \alpha_ {- i} \}}{t [ - t \alpha_ {i} (\alpha_ {- i} + s _ {- i , j} ^ {M M} \theta_ {- i}) + (2 t ^ {2} - \alpha_ {i} \alpha_ {- i}) (\alpha_ {i} + s _ {i , j} ^ {M M} \theta_ {i}) ]} & k = M M \\ 1 - \frac {2 C (\alpha_ {i} + \alpha_ {- i} + \theta_ {- i} (s _ {- i , j} ^ {S M} + s _ {- i , j} ^ {S M}) - 2 t)}{(\alpha_ {i} + \alpha_ {- i}) H + C (\alpha_ {i} + \alpha_ {- i} + 2 \theta_ {- i} s _ {- i , j} ^ {S M})} & k = S M \end{array} \right.,\tag{4}
$$

where $C = 6 t ^ { 2 } - \alpha _ { i } ^ { 2 } - 4 \alpha _ { i } \alpha _ { - i } - \alpha _ { - i } ^ { 2 }$ and $H = 2 t { \theta } _ { i } \Delta s _ { i , j } ^ { S M } +$ $\theta _ { - i } \Delta s _ { - i , j } ^ { S M } ( \alpha _ { i } + \alpha _ { - i } )$

Since user loyalty only exists in the case of multihoming, under the multi-multi-homing policy $( k =$ ????) , ?? = ?? or ?? and −?? = ?? or ?? accordingly; under the hybrid homing policy $( k = S M ) , i = Y$ is the multihoming side, and $- i = X$ is the single-homing side. Based on the impact of the platform’s service level on the degree of user participation under different homing policies (see Appendix Tables $\mathbf { A l - A } 2 )$ , we derive the following proposition.

Proposition 4: Impact of service level on user loyalty with MM homing policy:

(a) Non-positive self-influence effect: $\partial L _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } \big / \partial s _ { i , j } ^ { M M } \leq 0 ;$

(b) Non-positive intraside interplatform spillover effect: $\partial L _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } / \partial s _ { i , - j } ^ { M M } \leq 0 ;$

(c) Interside intraplatform spillover effect: $\partial L _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } / \partial s _ { - i , j } ^ { M M } \geq 0$ when users are in $\mathcal { F P } / \mathcal { P P }$ mode, and $\partial L _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } / \partial s _ { - i , j } ^ { M M } \leq 0$ when users are in $\mathcal { F F } / \mathcal { P F }$ mode;

(d) Non-negative interside interplatform spillover effect: $\partial L _ { i , j } ^ { M M * } / \partial s _ { - i , - j } ^ { M M } \geq 0 ;$ where $j \in \{ A , B \} , - j \in$ $\{ A , B \} , j \neq - j .$

Proposition 4 posits the influences of service levels on user loyalty when the platform implements the ???? policy that gives users freedom on both sides to choose either platform. For convenience, we take $i = X , - i = Y$ and $j = A , - j = B$ as examples to illustrate Proposition 4.

Proposition 4a shows a non-positive self-influence effect, i.e., the loyalty of users on side ?? of platform A will not increase with the service level on side ?? provided by this platform (i.e., platform A). In particular, $\stackrel { \textstyle - } { \partial } L _ { X , A } ^ { M M * } \big / \partial s _ { X , A } ^ { \dot { M } M } =$ 0 with $\mathcal { F } \# \left( \mathrm { i . e . , } \mathcal { F } \mathcal { F } \thinspace \mathrm { o r } \mathcal { F } \mathcal { P } \right)$ , and $\partial L _ { X , A } ^ { M M * } \big / \partial s _ { X , A } ^ { M M } < 0$ with $\mathcal { P } \# \left( \mathrm { i . e . , } \mathcal { P } \mathcal { F } \thinspace \mathrm { o r } \mathcal { P } \mathcal { P } \right)$ , suggesting that the self-influence effect depends on the degree of user participation on side ??, i.e., as long as the users on side X fully participating, the focal platform’s service to side X does not affect the loyalty of this side; as long as the users on side X partially participating, the focal platform’s service to side X decreases the loyalty of this side. According to Appendix Table $\mathbf { A } 2 ,$ the service level on side ?? provided by the focal platform, i.e., $s _ { X , A } ^ { M M }$ , will not affect the number of users who only belong to the focal platform $( \mathrm { i . e . , } n _ { X , A } ^ { M M * } )$ with ℱ# but increase $n _ { X , A } ^ { M M * }$ with $\mathcal { P \# }$ . However, in the participation mode of ℱ# , because the users on side ?? have already fully participated, i.e., $N _ { X , A } ^ { M M * } = 1$ increasing the service level on side ?? cannot attract more users. According to the definition of user loyalty, although the service level is rising, unchanged $n _ { X , A } ^ { M M * }$ and $N _ { X , A } ^ { M M * }$ will not affect user loyalty. On the other hand, in the participation mode of $\mathcal { P \# }$ , the users on side ?? partially join the market. If the focal platform provides a higher service level, side ?? will attract more users and only some of them will participate on platform A. Thus, the fraction of $n _ { X , A } ^ { M M * }$ and $N _ { X , A } ^ { M \mathbf { \hat { M } * } }$ may become smaller, thus reducing loyalty. It suggests that if the platform improves the service level on one side, it will attract more users on this side. However, the increased number of users includes multi-homers who belong to two platforms. Therefore, the focal platform needs to take action to convert the multi-homers into single-homers to increase its competitiveness.

Proposition 4b presents a non-positive intraside interplatform spillover effect, i.e., when the rival platform (i.e., platform B) offers a higher service level on side ??, the number of loyal users on the focal platform may be reduced.

Proposition 4c shows the opposite interside intraplatform spillover effect, which depends on user participation on side ?? . Specifically, when the focal platform provides a higher service level to side ${ \cal Y } ,$ user loyalty on side ?? could increase in the participation modes of $\# \mathcal { P } \left( \mathrm { i . e . , \mathcal { F P } \ o r \mathcal { P P } } \right)$ . The reason may be that the users on side ?? partially participate on the platform, and the increased service could attract more users to attend side ??. Through the network effect, the number of loyal users on side ?? of the focal platform may also increase. On the other hand, when the focal platform provides a higher service level to side ??, user loyalty on side ?? may decrease in the participation modes of #ℱ (i.e., ℱℱ or ??ℱ). When the focal platform takes actions to improve the service on side ?? , such actions may inadvertently reduce service quality on side $X ,$ either due to budget constraints or increased operational complexity for users on that side. For example, Airbnb planned to implement a new cancellation policy starting October 1, 2025, that removes the “strict” cancellation option and introduces a more flexible “limited” policy. An Airbnb spokesperson explained that “over 40 percent of guests say free cancellation is one of their top needs when choosing to book a stay” (Newsweek, 2025). While this change seems highly beneficial for travelers, many hosts have expressed disappointment and concern—particularly those managing high-value or seasonal listings booked months in advance—due to increased risks of last-minute cancellations (Durbin, 2025). Moreover, Airbnb now highlights listings that offer free cancellation beyond 48 hours in search results, potentially favoring more flexible listings in terms of visibility. Such a policy shift may disadvantage higherquality hosts with stricter policies and could even drive them to competing platforms (Trovato, 2025).

Proposition 4d suggests that when the rival platform provides higher service to side $Y \left( { \mathrm { i . e . , } } s _ { Y , B } ^ { M M } \right)$ , users on side ?? tend to exhibit increased loyalty to the focal platform $( \mathrm { i } . \mathrm { e } . , L _ { X , A } ^ { M M } )$ . This seemingly counterintuitive phenomenon can be explained by the rival platform’s potential resource reallocation. Specifically, an enhanced service offering for side ?? by the rival may necessitate a trade-off, potentially leading to reduced investment or increased friction for side ?? users on that platform. Consequently, these less satisfied ??-side users on the rival platform may find the focal platform relatively more appealing, thus strengthening their loyalty to it. A case study of the Swiss hotel sector shows that this phenomenon exists in the hotel industry’s interactions with online travel agencies (OTAs) such as Booking.com and Expedia (Balsiger et al., 2022). These platforms often invest heavily in improving the consumer experience—through user-friendly interfaces, personalized recommendations, and loyalty programs— to attract more bookings. However, these enhancements may come at a cost to the hotels themselves. For instance, OTAs may impose higher commission fees, enforce strict pricing parity clauses, or prioritize listings that adhere to specific platform standards, thereby increasing operational pressures on hotels. Such practices can lead to dissatisfaction among hotel operators, particularly smaller, family-run establishments that may lack the resources to comply with these demands. Consequently, some hotels choose to reduce their reliance on OTAs, instead focusing on direct booking channels or alternative platforms that offer more favorable terms.

Proposition 4 reveals the impacts of service level changes on user loyalty under the ???? homing policy. It underscores the importance of considering user participation modes in understanding those impacts. The aggravated effect observed in the $\mathcal { F F }$ or $\mathcal { P F }$ modes implies a delicate balance between service improvement and loyalty preservation. Enhancing service on one side may inadvertently lead to decreased user loyalty on both sides, signaling a need for strategic management to maintain overall user loyalty. Conversely, the doubleedged sword effect observed in the $\mathcal { F P }$ or $\mathcal { P P }$ modes introduces a strategic dilemma. Improving service on one side results in a trade-off, with user loyalty decreasing on that side but increasing on the other side. This trade-off requires platforms to carefully manage the potential shifts in user loyalty across sides due to service improvements. In summary, Proposition 4 provides a rich understanding of the complex relationship between service levels and user loyalty under the ???? homing policy. The insights from this proposition could guide platforms in optimizing their service strategies while considering the various user participation modes.

Proposition 5: Impact of service level on user loyalty with the hybrid homing policy $( i = Y$ is the multihoming side):

(a) Non-positive self-influence effect: $\partial L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } \big / \partial s _ { Y , j } ^ { S M } \leq 0 ;$

(b) Non-positive intraside interplatform spillover effect: $\partial L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { Y , - j } ^ { S M } \leq 0 .$ ;

(c) Non-negative interside intraplatform spillover effect: $\bar { \partial } L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { X , j } ^ { S M } \geq 0 $

(d) Non-positive interside interplatform spillover effect: $\partial L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { X , - j } ^ { S M } \leq 0$ , where $j \in \{ A , B \} , - j \in$ m $\{ A , B \} , j \neq - j$ , side ?? is single-homing, and side Y is multi-homing.

Proposition 5 illustrates the influence of service level on the platform’s user loyalty when the platform implements a hybrid homing policy. Proposition 5a shows that the users on the multi-homing side will stop being loyal to the focal platform (i.e., platform ??) if the platform increases the service level on that side. Appendix Table A3 show that $\partial p _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { Y , j } ^ { S M }$ is positive, suggesting that the price charged to side ?? will increase with the service level. Thus, loyalty will be reduced because the users who only join the focal platform are driven away by the increased price.

Propositions 5b and 5d suggest a negative interplatform spillover effect for intraside (i.e., $\partial L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { Y , - j } ^ { S M } \dot { \leq } 0 )$ and interside $( \mathrm { i . e . , } \quad \partial L _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } / \partial s _ { X , - j } ^ { S M } \leq 0 \quad ) .$ respectively. Whichever side of the rival platform increases the service level, side ?? of the rival platform will become more attractive directly through the increased service level or indirectly through the network effect. Then, the users on side ?? who only belong to the focal platform may switch from the focal platform to the rival platform (which can reduce $n _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * }$ and $N _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } ) , ^ { 1 3 }$ or choose the rival platform at the same time (which can reduce $n _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * }$ and unchanged $N _ { Y , j } ^ { S M * } )$ . No matter which situation happens, user loyalty on side ?? of the focal platform should decrease.

Proposition 5c presents that when the focal platform offers higher service for single-homers, the multi-homers become more loyal due to the positive network effect. It suggests that attention to the single-homing side could help to retain more loyal users on the multi-homing side. The platform needs to strike a balance between freedom and the service level offered to its users. Users who have multiple options (multi-homers) have ample freedom and may not prioritize service, while users who are limited to a single option (single-homers) have limited freedom and place a higher value on service quality. Improving service for single-homers can not only increase their satisfaction but also drive multi-homers to become more loyal due to the network effect. By strategically improving service on the single-homing side, the platform gains a competitive advantage. This approach aligns with the dynamics of user behavior, recognizing that the value placed on service quality can influence loyalty and user retention, especially under a hybrid homing policy.

In the literature on service management, divergent perspectives exist regarding whether service levels should surpass customer needs. While some assert the necessity, others contend that such efforts may yield only marginal impacts on customer loyalty (Dixon et al., 2010). Despite the disagreement, there is a consensus that enhancing service levels does not diminish customer loyalty. However, Propositions 4 and 5 introduce novel insights, suggesting that elevating service levels may generate dual influences on user loyalty on a two-sided platform, revealing how different levels of user freedom can yield distinct and sometimes opposing effects on interside spillover effects.

(1) Interside intraplatform spillover effects (Propositions 4c and 5c): On a platform characterized by a hybrid homing policy, where users enjoy partial freedom, Proposition 5c unveils a trend: Increasing the service level on one side of the focal platform enhances user loyalty on the other side of this platform. This suggests a positive interplay between service level and user loyalty when users have intermediate levels of freedom. Conversely, Proposition 4c portrays that on a platform with a complete freedom policy (????), user loyalty on one side of the focal platform decreases as the service level increases on the other side, particularly when users on that side have fully participated. This signifies that when users have complete freedom, an increased service level might not necessarily translate into improved user loyalty, highlighting the intricate relationship between service offerings and user commitment.

(2) Interside interplatform spillover effects (Propositions 4d and 5d): In our two-sided market consisting of duopoly digital platforms, user loyalty on one side of the focal platform is influenced by service level on the opposite side of its rival. Specifically, when both platforms adopt an ???? policy, user loyalty to one side of the focal platform will increase when the competing platform enhances service levels on the other side. Conversely, if both platforms adopt a hybrid policy, user loyalty to the multi-homing side of the focal platform will decrease when the competing platform improves service levels on the single-homing side. This reflects a particularly intriguing and counterintuitive dynamic: When the two-sided market becomes more open (i.e., under the ???? scenario), a platform’s effort to enhance its own service level may inadvertently boost user loyalty on the rival platform. At first glance, this appears to create a mutually reinforcing loop of quality improvements among competitors, suggesting positive spillovers. However, this dynamic is deceptive, as the rival platform’s gain in loyalty ultimately comes at the cost of the focal platform’s own user loyalty.

In short, the comparative insights from Propositions 4 and 5 enrich our understanding of the interplay between service levels, user loyalty, and the influence of user freedom across different homing policies.

Social welfare is the sum of platform profit and consumer surplus, as formulated in Equation (5).<sup>14</sup>

## 5.4 Social Welfare

$$
S W ^ {k *} = \sum_ {j \in \{A, B \}} \pi_ {j} ^ {k *} + \sum_ {i \in \{X, Y \}, j \in \{A, B \}} \int_ {0} ^ {N _ {i, j} ^ {k *}} U _ {i, j} ^ {k *} d N _ {i, j} ^ {k}\tag{5}
$$

To investigate the impact of network effects on the platform’s homing policy in terms of social welfare, we conducted a comparative analysis of the social welfare among different homing policies under different user participation modes, as illustrated in Figure 6. Under different combinations of $\alpha _ { X }$ and $\alpha _ { Y }$ , we compared the social welfare when platform ?? adopts three different policies: ????, ???? and ???? . Each region in the region plot is labeled with a specific policy, meaning that in this region, the social welfare under this policy is higher than that of the other two policies, i.e., the dominant policy. First, Figure 6a shows that when users fully participate on both sides (ℱℱ) , the ???? or ???? policy may be the optimal policy that can realize the highest social welfare. The absence of the ???? policy in Figure 6a means that ???? can never be the optimal policy to maximize social welfare. In most cases, the ???? policy achieves the maximum social welfare. Moreover, when the network effects on both sides are small, the ???? policy is optimal when the user participation mode is ℱ?? (as Figure 6b shows), while the ???? policy is optimal when the user participation mode is ??ℱ (as Figure 6c shows). When the network effect is at the medium level, the optimal homing policy depends on the user participation mode. However, regardless of the user participation modes, the ???? policy will consistently deliver more social welfare than other policies when network effects on both sides are very high.
